, the last equality is due to Proposition
5.1.7
.
But then

\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(z)\in U_{\sigma}(I_{S}R_{S})T_{\sigma}(I_{S}R_{S})U^{-}_{\sigma}(R_{S})\subset U_{\sigma}(I_{S}R_{S})T_{\sigma}(R_{S})U^{-}_{\sigma}(R_{S}),

as desired.

Now it only remains to prove the claim. To do this, we observe that
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(k_{S})\cap\pi(\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(H))
is a normal subgroup of
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(k_{S})
. However, the group
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(k_{S})
is simple over its center.
To see this, assume first that
\mathfrak{m}=\bar{\mathfrak{m}}
. In this case,
k_{S}=k_{\mathfrak{m}}
is a field, and our result follows from
[
52
, Theorem 34]
. Now, consider the case where
\mathfrak{m}\neq\bar{\mathfrak{m}}
. By Proposition
4.3.1
, we can deduce that
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(k_{S})
is isomorphic to
E_{\pi}(\Phi,k_{\mathfrak{m}})
. Therefore, applying
[
52
, Theorem 5]
, we conclude that
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(k_{S})
is simple over its center.
Thus, we can conclude that either
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(k_{S})\cap\pi(\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(H))\subset Z(G_{\sigma}(k_{S}))
or
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(k_{S})\cap\pi(\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(H))=E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(k_{S})
. Assume that
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(k_{S})\cap\pi(\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(H))\subset Z(G_{\sigma}(k_{S}))
. Then, by Proposition
5.1.8
, we have
\pi(\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(H))\subset T_{\sigma}(k_{S})
. Since
\pi(\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(H))
is non-central, there exists
h(\chi)\in\pi(\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(H))
with
\chi(\alpha)\neq 1
for some
\alpha\in\Phi
. But then
[h(\chi),x_{[\alpha]}(1)]=x_{[\alpha]}(\chi(\alpha)-1)\in\pi(\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(H))
, which is a contradiction to our assumption. Therefore, we must have
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(k_{S})\cap\pi(\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(H))=E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(k_{S})
, that is,
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(k_{S})\subset\pi(\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(H))
. ∎

Chapter 6
Normalizer of Twisted Chevalley Groups in a Larger Groups

Let
R
be a subring of a ring
S
. This chapter aims to describe the normalizers of
G_{\sigma}(R)
and
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)
in the larger group
G_{\sigma}(S)
. Throughout this chapter, we are going to assume that
\Phi_{\rho}\sim{}^{2}A_{n}\ (n\geq 3),{}^{2}D_{n}\ (n\geq 4)
or
{}^{2}E_{6}
.

6.1
The Tangent Algebra

In this section, we demonstrate how the Lie algebra associated with elementary Chevalley groups can also be reconstructed from the group itself. To achieve this, we introduce the concept of the tangent algebra, following the approach outlined by A. A. Klyachko in
[
37
]
.

6.1.1
The case of Chevalley Groups

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a standard simple Lie algebra of type
\Phi
over
\mathbb{C}
(i.e., it can be thought of as a subalgebra of
M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
).
Let
R
be a commutative ring with unity.
Consider Chevalley algebra
\mathcal{L}(\Phi,R)
and corresponding adjoint elementary Chevalley group
E(R)=E_{\text{ad}}(\Phi,R)
.

Let
R[t]
be the polynomial ring over
R
with indeterminate
t
. We call elements of
E(R[t])

curves
on
E
defined over
R
and refer to
E(R[t])
as the group of these curves.
Let
f(t)\in R[t]
be a polynomial. We define a map
\text{REP}_{f}:E(R[t])\rightarrow E(R[t])
that maps a curve
g(t)\in E(R[t])
to the curve
g(f(t))
. This map is a group endomorphism of
E(R[t])
. We call
g(f(t))
the
reparameterization
of the curve
g(t)
by the polynomial
f(t)
.

We now define a tangent space

T(E(R))=\{X\in M_{n}(R)\mid 1+tX+t^{2}Y\in E(R[t])\text{ for some }Y\in M_{n}(R[t])\}.

This set is an
R[E(R)]
-module, i.e., it is closed under the following operations:

1.

Addition:
(1+tX+o(t))(1+tY+o(t))=1+t(X+Y)+o(t)
;

2.

The scalar multiplication by
r\in R
:
\text{REP}_{rt}(1+tX+o(t))=1+rtX+o(t)
;

3.

The action of the group
E(R)
:
g(1+tX+o(t))g^{-1}=(1+tgXg^{-1}+o(t))
(henceforth, we define
g\circ X:=gXg^{-1}
).

Here,
o(t)
denotes a matrix of the form
t^{2}Z
, where
Z\in M_{n}(R[t])
.

Proposition 6.1.1

(
[
37
, Proposition 6]
)
.

\mathcal{L}(\Phi,R)=T(E(R))
.

Proof.

(\subseteq):
Consider a Chevalley basis
\{X_{\alpha},H_{\alpha_{i}}\mid\alpha\in\Phi\text{ and }\alpha_{i}\in\Delta\}
of
\mathcal{L}
. Since
T(E(R))
is an
R[E(R)]
-module, to prove
\mathcal{L}(\Phi,R)\subset T(E(R))
, it suffices to show that each basis element is contained in
T(E(R))
.
Clearly,
X_{\alpha}\in T(E(R))
because
x_{\alpha}(t)\in E(R[t])
. The remaining basis vectors
H_{\alpha_{i}}\in T(E(R))
because

H_{\alpha_{i}}=x_{\alpha_{i}}(1)\circ X_{-\alpha_{i}}+X_{\alpha_{i}}-X_{-\alpha_{i}}.

(\supseteq):

Let
X\in T(E(R))
. This means that
1+tX+o(t)\in E(R[t])
, and therefore it can be expressed as a product of elementary generators:

1+tX+o(t)=\prod_{j=1}^{m}x_{\alpha_{j}}(r_{j}t^{k_{j}}).

(6.1)

Clearly, we can assume that
k_{j}\in\{0,1\}
.
Let
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
be the subset of
\mathcal{J}=\{1,\dots,m\}
consisting of all
j
such that
k_{j}=0
.
Now, for all
j\in\mathcal{J}\setminus\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
, define

g_{j}=\prod_{i\in\mathcal{J}_{j}}x_{\alpha_{i}}(r_{i})\in E(R),

where
\mathcal{J}_{i}=\mathcal{J}^{\prime}\cap\{1,\dots,j\}
.
By putting
t=0
in equation (
6.1
), we obtain

g_{m}=\prod_{j\in\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}x_{\alpha_{j}}(r_{j})=1.

Using this, we can rewrite equation (
6.1
) as

1+tX+o(t)=\prod_{j\in\mathcal{J}\setminus\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}g_{j}x_{\alpha_{j}}(r_{j}t)g_{j}^{-1}.

By comparing both sides, we conclude that
X=\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}\setminus\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}g_{j}\circ(r_{j}X_{\alpha_{j}})\in\mathcal{L}(\Phi,R)
, as desired.
∎

6.1.2
The case of Twisted Chevalley Groups

We now replicate the argument in the context of twisted Chevalley groups.
Let
R
be a commutative ring with unity.
As before, assume there exists an automorphism
\theta
of
R
of order two.
This automorphism can be extended to the polynomial ring
R[t]
with the variable
t
as follows:
t\mapstochar\rightarrow t
and
r\mapstochar\rightarrow\theta(r)
for every
r\in R
. Consequently, the group
E^{\prime}_{\text{ad},\sigma}(\Phi,R[t])
is well-defined and contains
E^{\prime}_{\text{ad},\sigma}(\Phi,R)
as a subgroup.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a standard simple Lie algebra of type
\Phi
over
\mathbb{C}
. Consider the twisted Chevalley algebra
\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}(\Phi,R)
and the corresponding adjoint elementary twisted Chevalley group
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)=E^{\prime}_{\text{ad},\sigma}(\Phi,R)
.
As before, for
f(t)\in R_{\theta}[t]
, we define a map

\text{REP}_{f}:E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R[t])\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R[t])

that sends
g(t)\mapstochar\rightarrow f(g(t))
. This map is then an endomorphism of the group
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)
.
Define a tangent space

T(E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R))=\{X\in M_{n}(R)\mid 1+tX+t^{2}Y\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R[t])\text{ for some }Y\in M_{n}(R[t])\}.

This set is an
R_{\theta}[E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)]
-module, i.e., it is closed with respect to the following operations:

1.

Addition:
(1+tX+o(t))(1+tY+o(t))=1+t(X+Y)+o(t)
;

2.

The scalar multiplication by
r\in R_{\theta}
:
\text{REP}_{rt}(1+tX+o(t))=1+rtX+o(t)
;

3.

The action of the group
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)
:
g(1+tX+o(t))g^{-1}=(1+tgXg^{-1}+o(t))
(henceforth, we define
g\circ X:=gXg^{-1}
).

Observe that the map
\sigma:E_{\text{ad}}(\Phi,R)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow E_{\text{ad}}(\Phi,R)
can be extended to a
R_{\theta}
-linear map from
M_{n}(R)
onto itself, also denoted by the same symbol
\sigma
.

Proposition 6.1.2
.

\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}(\Phi,R)=T(E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)).

Proof.

(\subseteq):
Consider the
R_{\theta}
-basis of
\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}(\Phi,R)
as described in Section
4.2
. To establish the result, it suffices to show that each basis element lies in
T(E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R))
. We proceed as follows:

Case
[\alpha]\sim A_{1}
or
A_{1}^{2}
.

1.

X_{[\alpha]}^{+}\in T(E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R))
because
x_{[\alpha]}(t)\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(\Phi,R_{\theta}[t])
.

2.

X_{[\alpha]}^{-}(\mathrm{I})\in T(E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R))
because
x_{[\alpha]}(at)\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(\Phi,R_{\theta}[t])
(this is only in the case of
[\alpha]\sim A_{1}^{2}
).

3.

H_{[\alpha]}^{+}\in T(E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R))
because
H_{[\alpha]}^{+}=x_{[\alpha]}(1)\circ X_{-[\alpha]}^{+}+X_{[\alpha]}^{+}-X_{-[\alpha]}^{+}
.

4.

H_{[\alpha]}^{-}\in T(E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R))
because
H_{[\alpha]}^{-}=x_{[\alpha]}(1)\circ X_{-[\alpha]}^{-}(\mathrm{I})+X_{[\alpha]}^{-}(\mathrm{I})-X_{-[\alpha]}^{-}(\mathrm{I})
(this is only in the case of
[\alpha]\sim A_{1}^{2}
).

Case
[\alpha]\sim A_{2}
.

1.

X_{[\alpha]}^{+}\in T(E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R))
because
x_{[\alpha]}(t,t^{2}/2)\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(\Phi,R_{\theta}[t])
.

2.

X_{[\alpha]}^{-}(\mathrm{I})\in T(E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R))
because
x_{[\alpha]}(at,(at)^{2}/2)\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(\Phi,R_{\theta}[t])
.

3.

X_{[\alpha]}^{-}(\mathrm{II})\in T(E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R))
because
x_{[\alpha]}(0,at)\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(\Phi,R_{\theta}[t])
.

4.

H_{[\alpha]}^{+}\in T(E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R))
because
H_{[\alpha]}^{+}=x_{[\alpha]}(1,1/2)\circ X_{-[\alpha]}^{+}+\frac{1}{2}X_{[\alpha]}^{+}-X_{-[\alpha]}^{+}
.

5.

H_{[\alpha]}^{-}\in T(E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R))
because
H_{[\alpha]}^{-}=x_{[\alpha]}(1,1/2)\circ X_{-[\alpha]}^{-}+\frac{3}{2}X_{[\alpha]}^{-}(\mathrm{I})-X^{-}_{[\alpha]}(\mathrm{II})-X^{-}_{-[\alpha]}(\mathrm{I})
.

(\supseteq):

Let
X\in T(E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R))
, i.e.,
1+tX+o(t)\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R[t])
. Write it as a product of elementary generators:

1+tX+o(t)=\prod_{j=1}^{m}x_{[\alpha_{j}]}(f_{j}(t)),

(6.2)

where
f_{j}(t)=r_{j}\cdot t^{k_{j}}
if
[\alpha_{j}]\sim A_{1}
or
A_{1}^{2}
;
(r_{j}^{(1)}t^{k_{j}^{(1)}},r_{j}^{(2)}t^{k_{j}^{(2)}})
if
[\alpha]\sim A_{2}
.
Clearly, we can assume that
k_{j},k_{j}^{(1)},k_{j}^{(2)}\in\{0,1\}
.
Let
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
be the subset of
\mathcal{J}=\{1,\dots,m\}
consisting of all
j
such that
k_{j}=0
or
k_{j}^{(1)}=k_{j}^{(2)}=0
.
Now, for all
j\in\mathcal{J}\setminus\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
, define

g_{j}=\prod_{i\in\mathcal{J}_{j}}x_{[\alpha_{i}]}(f_{i}(t))=\prod_{i\in\mathcal{J}_{j}}x_{[\alpha_{i}]}(f_{i}(0))\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R),

where
\mathcal{J}_{i}=\mathcal{J}^{\prime}\cap\{1,\dots,j\}
.
By putting
t=0
in equation (
6.2
), we obtain

g_{m}=\prod_{j\in\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}x_{[\alpha_{j}]}(f_{j}(0))=1.

Using this, we can rewrite equation (
6.2
) as

1+tX+o(t)=\prod_{j\in\mathcal{J}\setminus\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}g_{j}x_{[\alpha_{j}]}(f_{j}(t))g_{j}^{-1}.

By comparing both sides, we conclude that
X=\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}\setminus\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}g_{j}\circ(r_{j}X^{\pm}_{[\alpha_{j}]})\in\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}(\Phi,R)
, as desired.
∎

6.2
Key Lemma

We now introduce a lemma that plays a crucial role in establishing the main result of this chapter.

Lemma 6.2.1
.

The set
E^{\prime}_{\pi,\sigma}(\Phi,R)
generates
M_{n}(R)
as an
R
-algebra, where
n
is the dimension of the corresponding representation
\pi
of Lie algebra
\mathcal{L}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be the
R
-subalgebra of
M_{n}(R)
generated by the set
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)
. To prove the lemma we must show that
\mathcal{M}=M_{n}(R)
.
We now assume that
\pi
is a minimal representation (the definition of minimal representation can be found in Section 3 of Chapter 3 in
[
66
]
). Therefore
\pi(X_{\alpha})^{3}=0
for all
\alpha\in\Phi
(note that we only consider the case where
\Phi\sim A_{n},D_{n}
, or
E_{6}
). In particular, we have

x_{\alpha}(t)=1+t\,\pi(X_{\alpha})+\frac{t^{2}\,\pi(X_{\alpha})^{2}}{2!}.

We now claim that the elements
\pi(X_{\alpha})\in\mathcal{M}
for every
\alpha\in\Phi
.
To see this, let
\alpha\in\Phi
and let
[\alpha]
denote the corresponding element in
\Phi_{\rho}
.
Just for our convenience, we use the notation
X_{\alpha}
to denote the linear map
\pi(X_{\alpha})
.

Suppose
[\alpha]\sim A_{1}
, then

x_{[\alpha]}(t)=x_{\alpha}(t)=1+t\,X_{\alpha}+\frac{t^{2}\,(X_{\alpha})^{2}}{2!}.

Then we can easily check that

X_{\alpha}=\frac{(x_{[\alpha]}(1)-x_{[\alpha]}(-1))}{2}.

Now suppose
[\alpha]\sim A_{1}^{2}
, then

\begin{split}x_{[\alpha]}(t)=x_{\alpha}(t)x_{\bar{\alpha}}(\bar{t})=1+t\,X_{\alpha}+\bar{t}\,X_{\bar{\alpha}}+\frac{t^{2}\,(X_{\alpha})^{2}}{2}+\frac{(\bar{t})^{2}\,(X_{\bar{\alpha}})^{2}}{2}+t\bar{t}\,(X_{\alpha}\,X_{\bar{\alpha}})\\
\frac{t(\bar{t})^{2}\,(X_{\alpha}\,(X_{\bar{\alpha}})^{2})}{2}+\frac{(t^{2})\bar{t}\,((X_{\alpha})^{2}\,X_{\bar{\alpha}})}{2}+\frac{(t\bar{t})^{2}\,((X_{\alpha})^{2}\,(X_{\bar{\alpha}})^{2})}{4}.\end{split}

A simple calculation on roots shows that

X_{\alpha}\,(X_{\bar{\alpha}})^{2}=(X_{\alpha})^{2}\,X_{\bar{\alpha}}=(X_{\alpha})^{2}\,(X_{\bar{\alpha}})^{2}=0.

Therefore,

x_{[\alpha]}(t)=1+\Big(t\,X_{\alpha}+\bar{t}\,X_{\bar{\alpha}}\Big)+\frac{1}{2}\Big(t\,X_{\alpha}+\bar{t}\,X_{\bar{\alpha}}\Big)^{2}.

Then we have

\displaystyle X_{\alpha}+X_{\bar{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(x_{[\alpha]}(1)-x_{[\alpha]}(-1)),\text{ and}

\displaystyle X_{\alpha}-X_{\bar{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2a}(x_{[\alpha]}(a)-x_{[\alpha]}(-a)).

Hence

\displaystyle X_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\Big(\big(x_{[\alpha]}(1)-x_{[\alpha]}(-1)\big)+a^{-1}\big(x_{[\alpha]}(a)-x_{[\alpha]}(-a)\big)\Big),\text{ and}

\displaystyle X_{\bar{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\Big(\big(x_{[\alpha]}(1)-x_{[\alpha]}(-1)\big)-a^{-1}\big(x_{[\alpha]}(a)-x_{[\alpha]}(-a)\big)\Big).

Finally suppose
[\alpha]\sim A_{2}
, then

\displaystyle x_{[\alpha]}(t,u)

\displaystyle=x_{\alpha}(t)x_{\bar{\alpha}}(\bar{t})x_{\alpha+\bar{\alpha}}(N_{\bar{\alpha},\alpha}u)

\displaystyle=1+\Big(t\,X_{\alpha}+\bar{t}\,X_{\bar{\alpha}}+N_{\bar{\alpha},\alpha}u\,X_{\alpha+\bar{\alpha}}\Big)+\Big(\frac{t^{2}}{2}\,(X_{\alpha})^{2}+\frac{(\bar{t})^{2}}{2}\,(X_{\bar{\alpha}})^{2}+\frac{u^{2}}{2}\,(X_{\alpha+\bar{\alpha}})^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+t\bar{t}\,(X_{\alpha}\,X_{\bar{\alpha}})+N_{\bar{\alpha},\alpha}tu\,(X_{\alpha}\,X_{\alpha+\bar{\alpha}})+N_{\bar{\alpha},\alpha}\bar{t}u\,(X_{\bar{\alpha}}\,X_{\alpha+\bar{\alpha}})\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\Big(N_{\bar{\alpha},\alpha}t\bar{t}u\,(X_{\alpha}\,X_{\bar{\alpha}}\,X_{\alpha+\bar{\alpha}})+\frac{t^{2}\bar{t}}{2}\,((X_{\alpha})^{2}\,X_{\bar{\alpha}})+\frac{t(\bar{t})^{2}}{2}\,(X_{\alpha}\,(X_{\bar{\alpha}})^{2})\Big).

Note that all the other terms in the expansion of the product
x_{\alpha}(t)x_{\bar{\alpha}}(\bar{t})x_{\alpha+\bar{\alpha}}(N_{\bar{\alpha},\alpha}u)
which does not appear in the above expression are
0
(for instance
X_{\alpha}\,(X_{\alpha+\bar{\alpha}})^{2}=0,(X_{\alpha})^{2}\,X_{\alpha+\bar{\alpha}}=0,
etc.).
First note that,

X_{\alpha+\bar{\alpha}}=\frac{N_{\bar{\alpha},\alpha}}{2a}\Big(X_{[\alpha]}(0,a)-x_{[\alpha]}(0,-a)\Big).

Now define

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{[\alpha]}(t,u)

\displaystyle=x_{[\alpha]}(t,u)-x_{[\alpha]}(-t,u)

\displaystyle=2(tX_{\alpha}+\bar{t}X_{\bar{\alpha}})+2u(tX_{\alpha}X_{\alpha+\bar{\alpha}}+\bar{t}X_{\bar{\alpha}}X_{\alpha+\bar{\alpha}})+t\bar{t}(t(X_{\alpha})^{2}X_{\bar{\alpha}}+\bar{t}X_{\alpha}(X_{\bar{\alpha}})^{2}).

Then

\displaystyle X_{\alpha}+X_{\bar{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{12}\big(8\mathcal{F}(1,1/2)-\mathcal{F}(2,2)\big),\text{ and }

\displaystyle X_{\alpha}-X_{\bar{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{12a}\big(8\mathcal{F}(a,-\frac{a^{2}}{2})-\mathcal{F}(2a,-2a^{2})\big).

Hence

\displaystyle X_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{24}\Big(\big(8\mathcal{F}(1,1/2)-\mathcal{F}(2,2)\big)+a^{-1}\big(8\mathcal{F}(a,-\frac{a^{2}}{2})-\mathcal{F}(2a,-2a^{2})\big)\Big),\text{ and }

\displaystyle X_{\bar{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{24}\Big(\big(8\mathcal{F}(1,1/2)-\mathcal{F}(2,2)\big)-a^{-1}\big(8\mathcal{F}(a,-\frac{a^{2}}{2})-\mathcal{F}(2a,-2a^{2})\big)\Big).

Therefore,
\pi(X_{\alpha})\in\mathcal{M}
for every
\alpha\in\Phi
.
In particular,
\pi(\mathcal{L}(R))\subset\mathcal{M}
.
Hence,
E_{\pi}(\Phi,R)\subset\mathcal{M}
.
By Lemma
2
of
[
17
]
, the set
E(R)
generates
M_{n}(R)
as an
R
-algebra. Therefore,
\mathcal{M}=M_{n}(R)
.
This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

6.3
Normalizer of
G_{\pi,\sigma}(\Phi,R)
and
E^{\prime}_{\pi,\sigma}(\Phi,R)
in
G_{\pi,\sigma}(\Phi,S)

The primary goal of this section is to establish the following theorem:

Theorem 6.3.1
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring with unity.
Let
G_{\pi,\sigma}(\Phi,R)
be a twisted Chevalley group of type
{}^{2}A_{n}\ (n\geq 3),{}^{2}D_{n}\ (n\geq 4)
or
{}^{2}E_{6}
, and let
E^{\prime}_{\pi,\sigma}(\Phi,R)
be its elementary subgroup.
Assume that
1/2\in R
and, if
\Phi_{\rho}\sim{}^{2}A_{2n}
, additionally assume that
1/3\in R
.
Furthermore, assume that there exists an invertible element
a\in R
such that
\theta(a)=-a
.
If
S
is a ring extension of
R
, then we have

N_{G_{\pi,\sigma}(\Phi,S)}(G_{\pi,\sigma}(\Phi,R))=N_{G_{\pi,\sigma}(\Phi,S)}(E^{\prime}_{\pi,\sigma}(\Phi,R)).

Moreover, if
G
is of adjoint type, we have

N_{G_{\text{ad},\sigma}(\Phi,S)}(G_{\text{ad},\sigma}(\Phi,R))=N_{G_{\text{ad},\sigma}(\Phi,S)}(E^{\prime}_{\text{ad},\sigma}(\Phi,R))=G_{\text{ad},\sigma}(\Phi,R).

Proof.

We begin by proving that
N_{G_{\pi,\sigma}(\Phi,S)}(G_{\pi,\sigma}(\Phi,R))=N_{G_{\pi,\sigma}(\Phi,S)}(E^{\prime}_{\pi,\sigma}(\Phi,R))
.
Since
E^{\prime}_{\pi,\sigma}(\Phi,R)
is a characteristic subgroup of
G_{\pi,\sigma}(\Phi,R)
(see Corollary
5.3.4
), it follows that

N_{G_{\pi,\sigma}(\Phi,S)}(G_{\pi,\sigma}(\Phi,R))\subset N_{G_{\pi,\sigma}(\Phi,S)}(E^{\prime}_{\pi,\sigma}(\Phi,R)).

For the reverse inclusion, let
g\in N_{G_{\pi,\sigma}(\Phi,S)}(E^{\prime}_{\pi,\sigma}(\Phi,R))
.
Thus,
gE^{\prime}_{\pi,\sigma}(\Phi,R)g^{-1}=E^{\prime}_{\pi,\sigma}(\Phi,R).

By the above lemma, we have
gM_{n}(R)g^{-1}=M_{n}(R)
.
This implies that
gGL_{n}(R)g^{-1}=GL_{n}(R)
.
In particular,
gG_{\pi,\sigma}(\Phi,R)g^{-1}\subset GL_{n}(R)
.
On the other hand,
gG_{\pi,\sigma}(\Phi,R)g^{-1}\subset G_{\pi,\sigma}(\Phi,S)
as
g\in G_{\pi,\sigma}(\Phi,S)
.
Therefore, we have

gG_{\pi,\sigma}(\Phi,R)g^{-1}\subset GL_{n}(R)\cap G_{\pi,\sigma}(\Phi,S)=G_{\pi,\sigma}(\Phi,R).

Thus,
g\in N_{G_{\pi,\sigma}(\Phi,S)}(G_{\pi,\sigma}(\Phi,R))
, which implies that

N_{G_{\pi,\sigma}(\Phi,S)}(E^{\prime}_{\pi,\sigma}(\Phi,R))\subset N_{G_{\pi,\sigma}(\Phi,S)}(G_{\pi,\sigma}(\Phi,R)),

as desired.

Now, it remains to prove that
N_{G_{\text{ad},\sigma}(\Phi,S)}(E^{\prime}_{\text{ad},\sigma}(\Phi,R))=G_{\text{ad},\sigma}(\Phi,R).

Clearly,

G_{\text{ad},\sigma}(\Phi,R)\subset N_{G_{\text{ad},\sigma}(\Phi,S)}(G_{\text{ad},\sigma}(\Phi,R))=N_{G_{\text{ad},\sigma}(\Phi,S)}(E^{\prime}_{\text{ad},\sigma}(\Phi,R)).

To establish the reverse inclusion, let
g\in N_{G_{\text{ad},\sigma}(\Phi,S)}(E^{\prime}_{\text{ad},\sigma}(\Phi,R))
.
Then, by definition,

gE^{\prime}_{\text{ad},\sigma}(\Phi,R)g^{-1}=E^{\prime}_{\text{ad},\sigma}(\Phi,R).

Using Proposition
6.1.2
, we conclude that

g(\text{ad}(\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}(\Phi,R)))g^{-1}=\text{ad}(\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}(\Phi,R)).

By “complexification”, we get

g(\text{ad}(\mathcal{L}(\Phi,R)))g^{-1}=\text{ad}(\mathcal{L}(\Phi,R)).

That is,
i_{g}
is an automorphism of the Lie algebra
\text{ad}(\mathcal{L}(\Phi,R))
.
Thus,
i_{g}
can be decomposed as
i_{g}=i_{g^{\prime}}\circ\delta
, where
g^{\prime}\in G_{\text{ad}}(\Phi,R)
and
\delta
is a graph automorphism (see Theorem 1 of
[
37
]
or Lemma 3 of
[
18
]
).
Consequently,
i_{g}
is an automorphism of
E_{\text{ad}}(\Phi,R)
that admits the same decomposition.
Since the intersection between the set of all graph automorphisms and the set of all inner automorphisms of
E_{\text{ad}}(\Phi,R)
is trivial (see
[
4
]
), we deduce that
\delta=\operatorname{id}
.
It follows that
g\cdot{g^{\prime}}^{-1}=\lambda\cdot 1_{n}\in G_{\text{ad}}(\Phi,S)
for some
\lambda\in S
.
Since there are no scalar matrices in the group of adjoint type (as the centre is trivial, see
[
6
]
), we conclude that
\lambda=1
.
Consequently,
g=g^{\prime}\in G_{\text{ad},\sigma}(\Phi,S)\cap G_{\text{ad}}(\Phi,R)=G_{\text{ad},\sigma}(\Phi,R)
.
Therefore,
N_{G_{\text{ad},\sigma}(\Phi,S)}(E^{\prime}_{\text{ad},\sigma}(\Phi,R))\subset G_{\text{ad},\sigma}(\Phi,R)
, as desired.
This completes the proof of Theorem
6.3.1
.
∎
